The following description:

* The Zkr extension adds the SSEED and USEED fields to the mseccfg CSR to control access to the seed CSR from U, S, or HS modes (see Privileged ISA specification).
* Implementations may implement mseccfg such that [s,u]seed is a read-only constant value 0.
* Software may discover if access to the seed CSR can be enabled in U and S mode by writing a 1 to [s,u]seed and reading back the result.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The Zkr extension adds the SSEED and USEED fields to the mseccfg CSR to control access to the seed CSR from U, S, or HS modes.
RULE 2: Software may discover if access to the seed CSR can be enabled in U and S mode by writing a 1 to [s,u]seed and reading back the result in the mseccfg register.

The following description:

The direct mseccfg.[s,u]seed option allows one to draw a security boundary around a component in relation to Sensitive Security Parameter (SSP) flows, even if that component is not in M mode.

This is helpful when implementing trusted enclaves. Such modules can enforce the entire key lifecycle from birth (in the entropy source) to death (zeroization) to occur without the key being passed across the boundary to external code.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The direct mseccfg.[s,u]seed option allows one to draw a security boundary around a component in relation to Sensitive Security Parameter (SSP) flows, even if that component is not in M mode.
RULE 2: Trusted enclaves utilizing the mseccfg.[s,u]seed option can enforce the entire key lifecycle from birth (in the entropy source) to death (zeroization) without the key being passed across the boundary to external code.
======================================================= END - 2 ========================================================

The following description:

* **mseccfg**:
    * Number: 0x747
    * Privilege: MRW
    * Name: mseccfg
    * Description: Machine security configuration register.

* **mseccfgh**:
    * Number: 0x757
    * Privilege: MRW
    * Name: mseccfgh
    * Description: Upper 32 bits of mseccfg , RV32 only.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The mseccfg register is the machine security configuration register.
RULE 2: The mseccfg register has the number 0x747.
RULE 3: The mseccfg register is accessible for machine-level read and write operations.
RULE 4: In the RV32 architecture, the mseccfg register has its upper 32 bits represented by the mseccfgh register.

The following description:

mseccfg is a 64-bit read/write register that controls security features. It exists if any extension that adds a field to mseccfg is implemented; otherwise, it is reserved.

**Zkr Extension Fields**
The Zkr extension adds the SSEED and USEED fields to the mseccfg CSR to control access to the seed CSR from modes less privileged than M.
*   When USEED is 0, access to the seed CSR in U-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
*   When USEED is 1, read-write access to the seed CSR from U-mode is allowed; all other types of accesses raise an illegal-instruction exception.
*   If Zkr or U-mode is not implemented, USEED is read-only zero.
*   When SSEED is 0, access to the seed CSR from S-/HS-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
*   When SSEED is 1, read-write access to the seed CSR from S-/HS-mode is allowed; all other types of accesses raise an illegal-instruction exception.
*   If Zkr or S-mode is not implemented, SSEED is read-only zero.

**H Extension Interaction**
When the H extension is also implemented, access to the seed CSR from an HS-qualified instruction leads to a virtual-instruction exception in VS and VU modes; all other types of accesses raise an illegal-instruction exception.

**Entropy Source Access Control (Table 20)**
Table 20. Entropy Source Access Control.
| Mode   | SSEED   | USEED   | Description                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
|--------|---------|---------|----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: mseccfg is a 64-bit read/write register that controls security features.
RULE 2: mseccfg exists if any extension that adds a field to mseccfg is implemented.
RULE 3: mseccfg is reserved if no extension that adds a field to mseccfg is implemented.
RULE 4: The Zkr extension adds the SSEED and USEED fields to the mseccfg CSR to control access to the seed CSR from modes less privileged than M.
RULE 5: When the USEED field in the mseccfg register is 0, access to the seed CSR in U-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 6: When the USEED field in the mseccfg register is 1, read-write access to the seed CSR from U-mode is allowed.
RULE 7: When the USEED field in the mseccfg register is 1, all other types of access to the seed CSR raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 8: If Zkr or U-mode is not implemented, the USEED field in the mseccfg register is read-only zero.
RULE 9: When the SSEED field in the mseccfg register is 0, access to the seed CSR from S-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 10: When the SSEED field in the mseccfg register is 0, access to the seed CSR from HS-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 11: When the SSEED field in the mseccfg register is 1, read-write access to the seed CSR from S-mode is allowed.
RULE 12: When the SSEED field in the mseccfg register is 1, read-write access to the seed CSR from HS-mode is allowed.
RULE 13: When the SSEED field in the mseccfg register is 1, all other types of access to the seed CSR raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 14: If Zkr or S-mode is not implemented, the SSEED field in the mseccfg register is read-only zero.
RULE 15: When the H extension is also implemented, access to the seed CSR from an HS-qualified instruction in VS mode leads to a virtual-instruction exception, a behavior related to the mseccfg register.
RULE 16: When the H extension is also implemented, access to the seed CSR from an HS-qualified instruction in VU mode leads to a virtual-instruction exception, a behavior related to the mseccfg register.
RULE 17: When the H extension is also implemented, all other types of access to the seed CSR raise an illegal-instruction exception, a behavior related to the mseccfg register.
======================================================= END - 4 ========================================================

The following description:

* If the Zicfilp extension is implemented, the mseccfg .MLPE field is reset to 0.
* Upon reset, the MML , MMWP , and RLB fields of the mseccfg register are set to 0, unless the platform mandates a different reset value.
* The USEED and SSEED fields of the mseccfg CSR must have defined reset values.
* The system must not allow them [USEED and SSEED] to be in an undefined state after reset.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: If the Zicfilp extension is implemented, the mseccfg .MLPE field is reset to 0.
RULE 2: Upon reset, the MML field of the mseccfg register is set to 0, unless the platform mandates a different reset value.
RULE 3: Upon reset, the MMWP field of the mseccfg register is set to 0, unless the platform mandates a different reset value.
RULE 4: Upon reset, the RLB field of the mseccfg register is set to 0, unless the platform mandates a different reset value.
RULE 5: The USEED field of the mseccfg CSR must have a defined reset value.
RULE 6: The SSEED field of the mseccfg CSR must have a defined reset value.
RULE 7: The system must not allow the USEED field of the mseccfg CSR to be in an undefined state after reset.
RULE 8: The system must not allow the SSEED field of the mseccfg CSR to be in an undefined state after reset.

The following description:

The mseccfg CSR contains the RLB, MMWP, and MML fields.

When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, the physical memory protection rules are summarized in the table below based on the bits on pmpcfg:

| Bits on pmpcfg | Bits on pmpcfg | Bits on pmpcfg | Bits on pmpcfg | Result (MMode) | Result (S/U Mode) |
|-----------------|-----------------|-----------------|-----------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| L               | R               | W               | X               | Inaccessible region (Access Exception)                                       | Inaccessible region (Access Exception)                                       |
| 0               | 0               | 0               | 1               | Access Exception                                                             | Execute-only region                                                          |
| 0               | 0               | 1               | 0               | Shared data region: Read/write on Mmode, read-only on S/U mode               | Shared data region: Read/write on Mmode, read-only on S/U mode               |
| 0               | 0               | 1               | 1               | Shared data region: Read/write for both Mand S/U mode                        | Shared data region: Read/write for both Mand S/U mode                        |
| 0               | 1               | 0               | 0               | Access Exception                                                             | Read-only region                                                             |
| 0               | 1               | 0               | 1               | Access Exception                                                             | Read/Execute region                                                          |
| 0               | 1               | 1               | 0               | Access Exception                                                             | Read/Write region                                                            |
| 0               | 1               | 1               | 1               | Access Exception                                                             | Read/Write/Execute region                                                    |
| 1               | 0               | 0               | 0               | Locked inaccessible region* (Access Exception)                               | Locked inaccessible region* (Access Exception)                               |
| 1               | 0               | 0               | 1               | Locked Execute-only region*                                                  | Access Exception                                                             |
| 1               | 0               | 1               | 0               | Locked Shared code region: Execute only on both Mand S/U mode.*              | Locked Shared code region: Execute only on both Mand S/U mode.*              |
| 1               | 0               | 1               | 1               | Locked Shared code region: Execute only on S/U mode, read/execute on Mmode.* | Locked Shared code region: Execute only on S/U mode, read/execute on Mmode.* |
| 1               | 1               | 0               | 0               | Locked Read-only region*                                                     | Access Exception                                                             |
| 1               | 1               | 0               | 1               | Locked Read/Execute region*                                                  | Access Exception                                                             |
| 1               | 1               | 1               | 0               | Locked Read/Write region*                                                    | Access Exception                                                             |
| 1               | 1               | 1               | 1               | Locked Shared data region: Read only on both Mand S/U mode.*                 | Locked Shared data region: Read only on both Mand S/U mode.*                 |
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The mseccfg CSR contains the RLB field.
RULE 2: The mseccfg CSR contains the MMWP field.
RULE 3: The mseccfg CSR contains the MML field.
RULE 4: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=1, W=1, X=1, the physical memory region is an inaccessible region (Access Exception) in both MMode and S/U Mode.
RULE 5: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=0, W=0, X=1, the physical memory region is an Access Exception in MMode and an execute-only region in S/U Mode.
RULE 6: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=0, W=1, X=0, the physical memory region is a shared data region: Read/write on Mmode, read-only on S/U mode in both MMode and S/U Mode.
RULE 7: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=0, W=1, X=1, the physical memory region is a shared data region: Read/write for both Mand S/U mode in both MMode and S/U Mode.
RULE 8: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=1, W=0, X=0, the physical memory region is an Access Exception in MMode and a read-only region in S/U Mode.
RULE 9: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=1, W=0, X=1, the physical memory region is an Access Exception in MMode and a read/execute region in S/U Mode.
RULE 10: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=1, W=1, X=0, the physical memory region is an Access Exception in MMode and a read/write region in S/U Mode.
RULE 11: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=1, W=1, X=1, the physical memory region is an Access Exception in MMode and a read/write/execute region in S/U Mode.
RULE 12: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=0, W=0, X=0, the physical memory region is a locked inaccessible region* (Access Exception) in both MMode and S/U Mode.
RULE 13: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=0, W=0, X=1, the physical memory region is a locked execute-only region* in MMode and an Access Exception in S/U Mode.
RULE 14: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=0, W=1, X=0, the physical memory region is a locked shared code region: Execute only on both Mand S/U mode.* in both MMode and S/U Mode.
RULE 15: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=0, W=1, X=1, the physical memory region is a locked shared code region: Execute only on S/U mode, read/execute on Mmode.* in both MMode and S/U Mode.
RULE 16: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=1, W=0, X=0, the physical memory region is a locked read-only region* in MMode and an Access Exception in S/U Mode.
RULE 17: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=1, W=0, X=1, the physical memory region is a locked read/execute region* in MMode and an Access Exception in S/U Mode.
RULE 18: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=1, W=1, X=0, the physical memory region is a locked read/write region* in MMode and an Access Exception in S/U Mode.
RULE 19: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=1, W=1, X=1, the physical memory region is a locked shared data region: Read only on both Mand S/U mode.* in both MMode and S/U Mode.

The following description:

The term xLPE is used to determine if forward-edge CFI using landing pads provided by the Zicfilp extension is enabled at a privilege mode.

When S-mode is implemented, for privilege mode M, xLPE is determined by mseccfg.MLPE.

Table 58. xLPE determination when S-mode is implemented
| Privilege Mode   | xLPE         |
|------------------|--------------|
| M                | mseccfg.MLPE |
| S or HS          | menvcfg.LPE  |
| VS               | henvcfg.LPE  |
| Uor VU           | senvcfg.LPE  |

When S-mode is not implemented, for privilege mode M, xLPE is determined by mseccfg.MLPE.

Table 59. xLPE determination when S-mode is not implemented
| Privilege Mode   | xLPE         |
|------------------|--------------|
| M                | mseccfg.MLPE |
| U                | menvcfg.LPE  |

The Zicfilp must be explicitly enabled for use at each privilege mode.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: When S-mode is implemented, for privilege mode M, xLPE is determined by mseccfg.MLPE, where xLPE is used to determine if forward-edge CFI using landing pads provided by the Zicfilp extension is enabled at a privilege mode.
RULE 2: When S-mode is not implemented, for privilege mode M, xLPE is determined by mseccfg.MLPE, where xLPE is used to determine if forward-edge CFI using landing pads provided by the Zicfilp extension is enabled at a privilege mode.

The following description:

* When the TW (Timeout Wait) bit in mstatus is set and WRS.NTO is executed in any privilege mode other than M mode, and it does not complete within an implementation-specific bounded time limit, the WRS.NTO instruction will cause an illegal-instruction exception.
* When executing in VS or VU mode, if the VTW bit is set in hstatus, the TW bit in mstatus is clear, and the WRS.NTO does not complete within an implementation-specific bounded time limit, the WRS.NTO instruction will cause a virtual-instruction exception.

============================================================

RESPONSE:

RULE 2: When executing in VS or VU mode, if the VTW bit is set in hstatus, the TW bit in mstatus is clear, and the WRS.NTO instruction does not complete within an implementation-specific bounded time limit, the WRS.NTO instruction will cause a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 3: WRS.NTO is not specified to cause an illegal-instruction exception if executed in Umode when the governing TW bit in mstatus is 0.

The following description:

mstatus[10:9] is the vector context status field, VS.
VS is shadowed in sstatus[10:9].
VS is defined analogously to the floating-point context status field, FS.

When mstatus.VS is set to Off, attempts to execute any vector instruction, or to access the vector CSRs, raise an illegal-instruction exception.

When mstatus.VS is set to Initial or Clean, executing any instruction that changes vector state, including the vector CSRs, will change mstatus.VS to Dirty.
Implementations may also change mstatus.VS from Initial or Clean to Dirty at any time, even when there is no change in vector state.

Accurate setting of mstatus.VS is an optimization.
Software will typically use VS to reduce context-swap overhead.

If mstatus.VS is Dirty, mstatus.SD is 1; otherwise, mstatus.SD is set in accordance with existing specifications.

The mstatus.VS field may exist even if misa.V is clear.
Allowing mstatus.VS to exist when misa.V is clear enables vector emulation and simplifies handling of mstatus.VS in systems with writable misa.V.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: mstatus[10:9] is the vector context status field, VS.
RULE 2: The VS field in mstatus is shadowed in sstatus[10:9].
RULE 3: The VS field in mstatus is defined analogously to the floating-point context status field, FS.
RULE 4: When mstatus.VS is set to Off, attempts to execute any vector instruction raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 5: When mstatus.VS is set to Off, attempts to access the vector CSRs raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 6: When mstatus.VS is set to Initial or Clean, executing any instruction that changes vector state, including the vector CSRs, will change mstatus.VS to Dirty.
RULE 7: Accurate setting of mstatus.VS is an optimization.
RULE 8: If mstatus.VS is Dirty, mstatus.SD is 1.
RULE 9: If mstatus.VS is not Dirty, mstatus.SD is set in accordance with existing specifications.
RULE 10: The mstatus.VS field may exist even if misa.V is clear.

* If the floating-point unit status field mstatus.FS is Off then any attempt to execute a vector floating-point instruction will raise an illegal-instruction exception.

The following description:

MIE and SIE are provided for M-mode and S-mode respectively. These bits are located in the low-order bits of mstatus, allowing them to be atomically set or cleared with a single CSR instruction.

When a hart is executing in privilege mode x, interrupts are globally enabled when x IE=1 and globally disabled when x IE=0. Interrupts for lower-privilege modes, w < x, are always globally disabled regardless of the setting of any global w IE bit for the lower-privilege mode. Interrupts for higher-privilege modes, y > x, are always globally enabled regardless of the setting of the global y IE bit for the higher-privilege mode. If supervisor mode is not implemented, then SIE is read-only 0.

To support nested traps, each privilege mode x that can respond to interrupts has a two-level stack of interrupt-enable bits and privilege modes. x PIE holds the value of the interrupt-enable bit active prior to the trap, and x PP holds the previous privilege mode. The x PP fields can only hold privilege modes up to x, so MPP is two bits wide and SPP is one bit wide.

When a trap is taken from privilege mode y into privilege mode x, x PIE is set to the value of x IE; x IE is set to 0; and x PP is set to y.

An MRET or SRET instruction is used to return from a trap in M-mode or S-mode respectively. When executing an x RET instruction, supposing x PP holds the value y, x IE is set to x PIE; the privilege mode is changed to y; x PIE is set to 1; and x PP is set to the least-privileged supported mode (U if U-mode is implemented, else M). If y ≠ M, x RET also sets MPRV=0.

x PP fields are WARL fields that can hold only privilege mode x and any implemented privilege mode lower than x. If privilege mode x is not implemented, then x PP must be read-only 0. M-mode software can determine whether a privilege mode is implemented by writing that mode to MPP then reading it back. If the machine provides only U and M modes, then only a single hardware storage bit is required to represent either 00 or 11 in MPP.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================

RULE 3: When a hart is executing in privilege mode x, interrupts are globally enabled when the x IE bit in the mstatus register is 1 and globally disabled when the x IE bit in the mstatus register is 0.
RULE 4: When a hart is executing in privilege mode x, interrupts for lower-privilege modes w < x are always globally disabled regardless of the setting of any global w IE bit in the mstatus register for the lower-privilege mode.
RULE 5: When a hart is executing in privilege mode x, interrupts for higher-privilege modes y > x are always globally enabled regardless of the setting of the global y IE bit in the mstatus register for the higher-privilege mode.

The following description:

The M-mode-disable-trap (MDT) bit is a WARL field introduced by the Smdbltrp extension. Upon reset, the MDT field is set to 1. For RV32, the MIE bit is in the mstatus register (while the MDT bit is in mstatush).

- When the MDT bit is set to 1 by an explicit CSR write, the MIE (Machine Interrupt Enable) bit is cleared to 0.
- For RV64, this clearing of the MIE bit occurs regardless of the value written, if any, to the MIE bit by the same write.
- The MIE bit can only be set to 1 by an explicit CSR write if the MDT bit is already 0 or, for RV64, is being set to 0 by the same write.

- When a trap is to be taken into M-mode, if the MDT bit is currently 0, it is then set to 1, and the trap is delivered as expected.
- If MDT is already set to 1, then this is an unexpected trap.
- When the Smrnmi extension is implemented, a trap caused by an RNMI is not considered an unexpected trap irrespective of the state of the MDT bit.
- A trap caused by an RNMI does not set the MDT bit.
- A trap that occurs when executing in M-mode with mnstatus.NMIE set to 0 is an unexpected trap.

- The MRET and SRET instructions, when executed in M-mode, set the MDT bit to 0.
- The MNRET instruction, provided by the Smrnmi extension, sets the MDT bit to 0 if the new privilege mode is not M.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========

RULE 3: For RV32, the MIE bit is in the mstatus register, while the MDT bit is in mstatush.

The following description:

For RV64 harts, the SXL and UXL fields in the mstatus register are WARL fields that control the value of XLEN for S-mode and U-mode, respectively. The encoding of these fields is the same as the MXL field of misa, shown in Table 11.

When MXLEN=32, the SXL and UXL fields do not exist, and SXLEN=32 and UXLEN=32.

When MXLEN=64, the behavior of the SXL and UXL fields in mstatus is as follows:
* If S-mode is not supported, then SXL is read-only zero.
* If S-mode is supported, then SXL is a WARL field that encodes the current value of SXLEN. In particular, an implementation may make SXL be a read-only field whose value always ensures that SXLEN=MXLEN.
* If U-mode is not supported, then UXL is read-only zero.
* If U-mode is supported, then UXL is a WARL field that encodes the current value of UXLEN. In particular, an implementation may make UXL be a read-only field whose value always ensures that UXLEN=MXLEN or UXLEN=SXLEN.

If S-mode is implemented, the set of legal values that the UXL field in mstatus may assume excludes those that would cause UXLEN to be greater than SXLEN.
============================================================
=========

=========

RULE 2: For RV64 harts, the UXL field in the mstatus register is a WARL field that controls the value of XLEN for U-mode.
RULE 3: The encoding of the SXL field in the mstatus register is the same as the MXL field of misa.
RULE 4: The encoding of the UXL field in the mstatus register is the same as the MXL field of misa.

RULE 7: When MXLEN=64 and S-mode is not supported, the SXL field in the mstatus register is read-only zero.

The following description:

**MPRV (Modify PRiVilege) bit**
* The MPRV bit modifies the effective privilege mode, i.e., the privilege level at which explicit memory accesses execute.
* When MPRV=0, explicit memory accesses behave as normal, using the translation and protection mechanisms of the current privilege mode.
* When MPRV=1, load and store memory addresses are translated and protected, and endianness is applied, as though the current privilege mode were set to MPP.
* Instruction address-translation and protection are unaffected by the setting of MPRV.
* MPRV is read-only 0 if U-mode is not supported.
* An MRET or SRET instruction that changes the privilege mode to a mode less privileged than M also sets MPRV=0.
* When MPRV=1, load and store memory addresses are treated as though the current XLEN were set to MPP's XLEN, following the rules in Section 3.1.6.3.
* MPRV obviates the need to perform address translation in software.

**MXR (Make eXecutable Readable) bit**
* The MXR bit modifies the privilege with which loads access virtual memory.
* When MXR=0, only loads from pages marked readable (R=1 in Figure 67) will succeed.
* When MXR=1, loads from pages marked either readable or executable (R=1 or X=1) will succeed.
* MXR has no effect when pagebased virtual memory is not in effect.
* MXR is read-only 0 if S-mode is not supported.
* MXR allows instruction words to be loaded from pages marked execute-only.

**SUM (permit Supervisor User Memory access) bit**
* The SUM bit modifies the privilege with which S-mode loads and stores access virtual memory.
* When SUM=0, S-mode memory accesses to pages that are accessible by Umode (U=1 in Figure 67) will fault.
* When SUM=1, these accesses are permitted.
* SUM has no effect when page-based virtual memory is not in effect.
* SUM is ordinarily ignored when not executing in S-mode, it is in effect when MPRV=1 and MPP=S.
* SUM is read-only 0 if S-mode is not supported or if satp.MODE is read-only 0.

**General MXR and SUM Behavior**
* The MXR and SUM mechanisms only affect the interpretation of permissions encoded in page-table entries.
* They [MXR and SUM] have no impact on whether access-fault exceptions are raised due to PMAs or PMP.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The MPRV bit in the mstatus register modifies the effective privilege mode, which is the privilege level at which explicit memory accesses execute.
RULE 2: When the MPRV bit in the mstatus register is 0, explicit memory accesses behave as normal, using the translation and protection mechanisms of the current privilege mode.
RULE 3: When the MPRV bit in the mstatus register is 1, load and store memory addresses are translated and protected, and endianness is applied, as though the current privilege mode were set to MPP.
RULE 4: Instruction address-translation and protection are unaffected by the setting of the MPRV bit in the mstatus register.
RULE 5: The MPRV bit in the mstatus register is read-only 0 if U-mode is not supported.
RULE 6: An MRET or SRET instruction that changes the privilege mode to a mode less privileged than M also sets the MPRV bit in the mstatus register to 0.
RULE 7: When the MPRV bit in the mstatus register is 1, load and store memory addresses are treated as though the current XLEN were set to MPP's XLEN.
RULE 8: The MPRV bit in the mstatus register obviates the need to perform address translation in software.
RULE 9: The MXR bit in the mstatus register modifies the privilege with which loads access virtual memory.
RULE 10: When the MXR bit in the mstatus register is 0, only loads from pages marked readable will succeed.
RULE 11: When the MXR bit in the mstatus register is 1, loads from pages marked either readable or executable will succeed.
RULE 12: The MXR bit in the mstatus register has no effect when page-based virtual memory is not in effect.
RULE 13: The MXR bit in the mstatus register is read-only 0 if S-mode is not supported.
RULE 14: The MXR bit in the mstatus register allows instruction words to be loaded from pages marked execute-only.
RULE 15: The SUM bit in the mstatus register modifies the privilege with which S-mode loads and stores access virtual memory.
RULE 16: When the SUM bit in the mstatus register is 0, S-mode memory accesses to pages that are accessible by Umode will fault.
RULE 17: When the SUM bit in the mstatus register is 1, S-mode memory accesses to pages that are accessible by Umode are permitted.
RULE 18: The SUM bit in the mstatus register has no effect when page-based virtual memory is not in effect.
RULE 19: The SUM bit in the mstatus register is ordinarily ignored when not executing in S-mode.
RULE 20: The SUM bit in the mstatus register is in effect when MPRV=1 and MPP=S.
RULE 21: The SUM bit in the mstatus register is read-only 0 if S-mode is not supported.
RULE 22: The SUM bit in the mstatus register is read-only 0 if satp.MODE is read-only 0.
RULE 23: The MXR and SUM bits in the mstatus register only affect the interpretation of permissions encoded in page-table entries.
RULE 24: The MXR and SUM bits in the mstatus register have no impact on whether access-fault exceptions are raised due to PMAs or PMP.
======================================================= END - 19 =======================================================

The following description:

The MBE, SBE, and UBE bits in mstatus and mstatush are WARL fields that control the endianness of memory accesses other than instruction fetches. Instruction fetches are always little-endian.

* MBE controls whether non-instruction-fetch memory accesses made from M-mode (assuming mstatus.MPRV=0) are little-endian (MBE=0) or big-endian (MBE=1).
* If S-mode is not supported, SBE is read-only 0. Otherwise, SBE controls whether explicit load and store memory accesses made from S-mode are little-endian (SBE=0) or big-endian (SBE=1).
* If U-mode is not supported, UBE is read-only 0. Otherwise, UBE controls whether explicit load and store memory accesses made from U-mode are little-endian (UBE=0) or big-endian (UBE=1).
* For implicit accesses to supervisor-level memory management data structures, such as page tables, endianness is always controlled by SBE.
* If any such data structures remain in use across a change to SBE, M-mode software must follow such a change to SBE by executing an SFENCE.VMA instruction with rs1 = x0 and rs2 = x0.
* If S-mode is supported, an implementation may make SBE be a read-only copy of MBE.
* If U-mode is supported, an implementation may make UBE be a read-only copy of either MBE or SBE.
* An implementation supports only little-endian memory accesses if fields MBE, SBE, and UBE are all read-only 0.
* An implementation supports only big-endian memory accesses (aside from instruction fetches) if MBE is read-only 1 and SBE and UBE are each read-only 1 when S-mode and U-mode are supported.

The correspondence between addresses and byte locations is fixed and not affected by any endianness mode. Rather, the applicable endianness mode determines the order of mapping between memory bytes and a multibyte quantity (halfword, word, etc.).

Endianness control has been defined so as to permit, for instance, an OS of one endianness to execute user-mode programs of the opposite endianness.

RISC-V instructions are uniformly little-endian to decouple instruction encoding from the current endianness settings. In big-endian mode, software must account for the fact that explicit loads and stores have endianness opposite that of instructions, for example by swapping byte order after loads and before stores.
============================================================

RULE 3: The UBE bit in mstatus is a WARL field that controls the endianness of memory accesses other than instruction fetches.

The following description:

The TVM (Trap Virtual Memory) bit is a WARL field that supports intercepting supervisor virtual-memory management operations.
* When TVM=1, attempts to read or write the satp CSR or execute an SFENCE.VMA or SINVAL.VMA instruction while executing in S-mode will raise an illegal-instruction exception.
* When TVM=0, these operations are permitted in S-mode.
* TVM is read-only 0 when S-mode is not supported.

The TW (Timeout Wait) bit is a WARL field that supports intercepting the WFI instruction (see Section 3.3.3).
* When TW=0, the WFI instruction may execute in modes less privileged than M when not prevented for some other reason.
* When TW=1, then if WFI is executed in any less-privileged mode, and it does not complete within an implementation-specific, bounded time limit, the WFI instruction causes an illegalinstruction exception.
* An implementation may have WFI always raise an illegal-instruction exception in modes less privileged than M when TW=1, even if there are pending globally-disabled interrupts when the instruction is executed.
* TW is read-only 0 when there are no modes less privileged than M.
* When S-mode is implemented, then executing WFI in U-mode causes an illegal-instruction exception, regardless of the value of the TW bit, unless the instruction completes within an implementation-specific, bounded time limit.

The TSR (Trap SRET) bit is a WARL field that supports intercepting the supervisor exception return instruction, SRET.
* When TSR=1, attempts to execute SRET while executing in S-mode will raise an illegalinstruction exception.
* When TSR=0, this operation is permitted in S-mode.
* TSR is read-only 0 when S-mode is not supported.

The following description:

The mstatus register contains the FS[1:0], VS[1:0], and XS[1:0] fields, as well as the SD bit.

*   **FS[1:0]**: Encodes the status of the floating-point unit state, including the floating-point registers f0 - f31 and the CSRs fcsr, frm, and fflags.
*   **VS[1:0]**: Encodes the status of the vector extension state, including the vector registers v0 - v31 and the CSRs vcsr, vxrm, vxsat, vstart, vl, vtype, and vlenb.
*   **XS[1:0]**: Encodes the status of additional user-mode extensions and associated state. It represents a summary of all extensions' status as shown in Table 13 and effectively reports the maximum status value across all user-extension status fields, though individual extensions can use a different encoding than XS.
*   **SD bit**: A read-only bit that summarizes whether either the FS, VS, or XS fields signal the presence of some dirty state that will require saving extended user context to memory.
    *   SD=(FS==0b11 OR XS==0b11 OR VS==0b11).
    *   If FS, XS, and VS are all readonly zero, then SD is also always zero.

**Table 13. Encoding of FS[1:0], VS[1:0], and XS[1:0] status fields**

| Status | FS and VS Meaning | XS Meaning |
|--------|-------------------|------------|
| 0      | Off               | All off    |
| 1      | Initial           | None dirty or clean, some on |
| 2      | Clean             | None dirty, some clean |
| 3      | Dirty             | Some dirty |

**Register Behavior and Constraints**

*   If the F extension is implemented, the FS field shall not be read-only zero.
*   If neither the F extension nor S-mode is implemented, then FS is read-only zero.
*   If S-mode is implemented but the F extension is not, FS may optionally be read-only zero.
*   If the v registers are implemented, the VS field shall not be read-only zero.
*   If neither the v registers nor S-mode is implemented, then VS is read-only zero.
*   If S-mode is implemented but the v registers are not, VS may optionally be read-only zero.
*   In harts without additional user extensions requiring new state, the XS field is read-only zero.
*   The FS and XS fields are read by the privileged code before saving the context.
*   The FS field is set directly by privileged code when resuming a user context.
*   The XS field is set indirectly by writing to the status register of the individual extensions.
*   The status fields (FS, VS, and XS) will also be updated during execution of instructions, regardless of privilege mode.
*   Machine and Supervisor modes share a single copy of the FS, VS, and XS bits.

**Status-Dependent Architectural Behavior**

*   When an extension's status is set to Off, any instruction that attempts to read or write the corresponding state will cause an illegal-instruction exception.
*   When the status is Initial, the corresponding state should have an initial constant value.
*   When the status is Clean, the corresponding state is potentially different from the initial value, but matches the last value stored on a context swap.
*   When the status is Dirty, the corresponding state has potentially been modified since the last context save.

**Context Save and Restore Rules**

*   During a context save, the responsible privileged code need only write out the corresponding state if its status is Dirty, and can then reset the extension's status to Clean.
*   During a context restore, the context need only be loaded from memory if the status is Clean (it should never be Dirty at restore).
*   If the status is Initial, the context must be set to an initial constant value on context restore to avoid a security hole, but this can be done without accessing memory.

**Additional Behavior and Implementation Details**

*   Changing the setting of FS has no effect on the contents of the floating-point register state. Setting FS=Off does not destroy the state, nor does setting FS=Initial clear the contents.
*   The setting of VS has no effect on the contents of the vector register state.
*   Other extensions, however, might not preserve state when set to Off.
*   Implementations may choose to track the dirtiness of the floating-point register state imprecisely by reporting the state to be dirty even when it has not been modified.
*   On some implementations, some instructions that do not mutate the floating-point state may cause the state to transition from Initial or Clean to Dirty.
*   On other implementations, dirtiness might not be tracked at all, in which case the valid FS states are Off and Dirty, and an attempt to set FS to Initial or Clean causes it to be set to Dirty.
*   This definition of FS does not disallow setting FS to Dirty as a result of errant speculation. Some platforms may choose to disallow speculatively writing FS to close a potential side channel.
*   If an instruction explicitly or implicitly writes a floating-point register or the fcsr but does not alter its contents, and FS=Initial or FS=Clean, it is implementation-defined whether FS transitions to Dirty.
*   Implementations may choose to track the dirtiness of the vector register state in an analogous imprecise fashion, including possibly setting VS to Dirty when software attempts to set VS=Initial or VS=Clean.
*   When VS=Initial or VS=Clean, it is implementation-defined whether an instruction that writes a vector register or vector CSR but does not alter its contents causes VS to transition to Dirty.

**Table 14. FS, VS, and XS state transitions.**

| Current State Action | Off | Initial | Clean | Dirty |
|----------------------|-----|---------|-------|-------|
| At context save in privileged code | At context save in privileged code | At context save in privileged code | At context save in privileged code | At context save in privileged code |
| Save state? Next state | No Off | No Initial | No Clean | Yes Clean |
| At context restore in privileged code | At context restore in privileged code | At context restore in privileged code | At context restore in privileged code | At context restore in privileged code |
| Restore state? Next state | No Off | Yes, to initial Initial | Yes, from memory Clean | N/A N/A |
| Execute instruction to read state | Execute instruction to read state | Execute instruction to read state | Execute instruction to read state | Execute instruction to read state |
| Action? Next state | Exception Off | Execute Initial | Execute Clean | Execute Dirty |
| Execute instruction that possibly modifies state, including configuration | Execute instruction that possibly modifies state, including configuration | Execute instruction that possibly modifies state, including configuration | Execute instruction that possibly modifies state, including configuration | Execute instruction that possibly modifies state, including configuration |
| Action? Next state | Exception Off | Execute Dirty | Execute Dirty | Execute Dirty |
| Execute instruction to unconfigure unit | Execute instruction to unconfigure unit | Execute instruction to unconfigure unit | Execute instruction to unconfigure unit | Execute instruction to unconfigure unit |
| Action? Next state | Exception Off | Execute Initial | Execute Initial | Execute Initial |
| Execute instruction to disable unit | Execute instruction to disable unit | Execute instruction to disable unit | Execute instruction to disable unit | Execute instruction to disable unit |
| Action? Next state | Execute Off | Execute Off | Execute Off | Execute Off |
| Execute instruction to enable unit | Execute instruction to enable unit | Execute instruction to enable unit | Execute instruction to enable unit | Execute instruction to enable unit |
| Action? Next state | Execute Initial | Execute Initial | Execute Initial | Execute Initial |

RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The mstatus register contains the FS[1:0], VS[1:0], and XS[1:0] fields, as well as the SD bit.
RULE 2: The FS[1:0] field in the mstatus register encodes the status of the floating-point unit state, including the floating-point registers f0 - f31 and the CSRs fcsr, frm, and fflags.
RULE 3: The VS[1:0] field in the mstatus register encodes the status of the vector extension state, including the vector registers v0 - v31 and the CSRs vcsr, vxrm, vxsat, vstart, vl, vtype, and vlenb.
RULE 4: The XS[1:0] field in the mstatus register encodes the status of additional user-mode extensions and associated state.
RULE 5: The XS[1:0] field in the mstatus register represents a summary of all extensions' status and effectively reports the maximum status value across all user-extension status fields, though individual extensions can use a different encoding than XS.
RULE 6: The SD bit in the mstatus register is a read-only bit that summarizes whether either the FS, VS, or XS fields signal the presence of some dirty state that will require saving extended user context to memory.
RULE 7: The SD bit in the mstatus register is set to 1 if any of the FS, VS, or XS fields are 0b11.
RULE 8: If the FS, XS, and VS fields in the mstatus register are all read-only zero, then the SD bit in the mstatus register is also always zero.
RULE 9: In the mstatus register, an FS[1:0] value of 0 signifies that the floating-point unit is Off.
RULE 10: In the mstatus register, an FS[1:0] value of 1 signifies that the floating-point unit is Initial.
RULE 11: In the mstatus register, an FS[1:0] value of 2 signifies that the floating-point unit is Clean.
RULE 12: In the mstatus register, an FS[1:0] value of 3 signifies that the floating-point unit is Dirty.
RULE 13: In the mstatus register, a VS[1:0] value of 0 signifies that the vector extension is Off.
RULE 14: In the mstatus register, a VS[1:0] value of 1 signifies that the vector extension is Initial.
RULE 15: In the mstatus register, a VS[1:0] value of 2 signifies that the vector extension is Clean.
RULE 16: In the mstatus register, a VS[1:0] value of 3 signifies that the vector extension is Dirty.
RULE 17: In the mstatus register, an XS[1:0] value of 0 signifies that all additional user-mode extensions are off.
RULE 18: In the mstatus register, an XS[1:0] value of 1 signifies that none of the additional user-mode extensions are dirty or clean, and some are on.
RULE 19: In the mstatus register, an XS[1:0] value of 2 signifies that none of the additional user-mode extensions are dirty, and some are clean.
RULE 20: In the mstatus register, an XS[1:0] value of 3 signifies that some additional user-mode extensions are dirty.
RULE 21: If the F extension is implemented, the FS field in the mstatus register shall not be read-only zero.
RULE 22: If neither the F extension nor S-mode is implemented, then the FS field in the mstatus register is read-only zero.
RULE 23: If S-mode is implemented but the F extension is not, the FS field in the mstatus register may optionally be read-only zero.
RULE 24: If the v registers are implemented, the VS field in the mstatus register shall not be read-only zero.
RULE 25: If neither the v registers nor S-mode is implemented, then the VS field in the mstatus register is read-only zero.
RULE 26: If S-mode is implemented but the v registers are not, the VS field in the mstatus register may optionally be read-only zero.
RULE 27: In harts without additional user extensions requiring new state, the XS field in the mstatus register is read-only zero.
RULE 28: The FS and XS fields in the mstatus register are read by the privileged code before saving the context.
RULE 29: The FS field in the mstatus register is set directly by privileged code when resuming a user context.
RULE 30: The XS field in the mstatus register is set indirectly by writing to the status register of the individual extensions.
RULE 31: The FS, VS, and XS fields in the mstatus register will be updated during execution of instructions, regardless of privilege mode.
RULE 32: Machine and Supervisor modes share a single copy of the FS, VS, and XS bits in the mstatus register.
RULE 33: When an extension's status in the mstatus register is set to Off, any instruction that attempts to read or write the corresponding state will cause an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 34: When the status in the mstatus register is Initial, the corresponding state should have an initial constant value.
RULE 35: When the status in the mstatus register is Clean, the corresponding state is potentially different from the initial value, but matches the last value stored on a context swap.
RULE 36: When the status in the mstatus register is Dirty, the corresponding state has potentially been modified since the last context save.
RULE 37: During a context save, the responsible privileged code need only write out the corresponding state if its status in the mstatus register is Dirty, and can then reset the extension's status in the mstatus register to Clean.
RULE 38: During a context restore, the context need only be loaded from memory if the status in the mstatus register is Clean (it should never be Dirty at restore).
RULE 39: If the status in the mstatus register is Initial, the context must be set to an initial constant value on context restore to avoid a security hole, but this can be done without accessing memory.
RULE 40: Changing the setting of FS in the mstatus register has no effect on the contents of the floating-point register state.
RULE 41: Setting FS=Off in the mstatus register does not destroy the state, and setting FS=Initial in the mstatus register does not clear the contents.
RULE 42: The setting of VS in the mstatus register has no effect on the contents of the vector register state.
RULE 43: Other extensions might not preserve state when set to Off in the mstatus register.
RULE 44: Implementations may choose to track the dirtiness of the floating-point register state imprecisely by reporting the state to be dirty in the mstatus register even when it has not been modified.
RULE 45: On some implementations, some instructions that do not mutate the floating-point state may cause the state to transition from Initial or Clean to Dirty in the mstatus register.
RULE 46: On other implementations, dirtiness might not be tracked at all, in which case the valid FS states in the mstatus register are Off and Dirty, and an attempt to set FS to Initial or Clean causes it to be set to Dirty.
RULE 47: The definition of FS in the mstatus register does not disallow setting FS to Dirty as a result of errant speculation.
RULE 48: Some platforms may choose to disallow speculatively writing FS to Dirty in the mstatus register to close a potential side channel.
RULE 49: If an instruction explicitly or implicitly writes a floating-point register or the fcsr but does not alter its contents, and FS=Initial or FS=Clean in the mstatus register, it is implementation-defined whether FS transitions to Dirty.
RULE 50: Implementations may choose to track the dirtiness of the vector register state in an imprecise fashion, including possibly setting VS to Dirty in the mstatus register when software attempts to set VS=Initial or VS=Clean.
RULE 51: When VS=Initial or VS=Clean in the mstatus register, it is implementation-defined whether an instruction that writes a vector register or vector CSR but does not alter its contents causes VS to transition to Dirty.
RULE 52: During a context save in privileged code, if the status in the mstatus register is Dirty, the next state is Clean and the state is saved.
RULE 53: During a context save in privileged code, if the status in the mstatus register is Off, Initial, or Clean, the next state remains Off, Initial, or Clean, respectively, and the state is not saved.
RULE 54: During a context restore in privileged code, if the status in the mstatus register is Initial, the next state is Initial and the context is set to the initial value.
RULE 55: During a context restore in privileged code, if the status in the mstatus register is Clean, the next state is Clean and the context is loaded from memory.
RULE 56: During a context restore in privileged code, if the status in the mstatus register is Off, the next state remains Off and no context is restored.
RULE 57: When executing an instruction to read state, if the status in the mstatus register is Off, the next state is Off and an exception occurs.
RULE 58: When executing an instruction to read state, if the status in the mstatus register is Initial, the next state is Initial and the instruction executes.
RULE 59: When executing an instruction to read state, if the status in the mstatus register is Clean, the next state is Clean and the instruction executes.
RULE 60: When executing an instruction to read state, if the status in the mstatus register is Dirty, the next state is Dirty and the instruction executes.
RULE 61: When executing an instruction that possibly modifies state, if the status in the mstatus register is Off, the next state is Off and an exception occurs.
RULE 62: When executing an instruction that possibly modifies state, if the status in the mstatus register is Initial, the next state is Dirty and the instruction executes.
RULE 63: When executing an instruction that possibly modifies state, if the status in the mstatus register is Clean, the next state is Dirty and the instruction executes.
RULE 64: When executing an instruction that possibly modifies state, if the status in the mstatus register is Dirty, the next state is Dirty and the instruction executes.
RULE 65: When executing an instruction to unconfigure unit, if the status in the mstatus register is Off, the next state is Off and an exception occurs.
RULE 66: When executing an instruction to unconfigure unit, if the status in the mstatus register is Initial, the next state is Initial and the instruction executes.
RULE 67: When executing an instruction to unconfigure unit, if the status in the mstatus register is Clean, the next state is Initial and the instruction executes.
RULE 68: When executing an instruction to unconfigure unit, if the status in the mstatus register is Dirty, the next state is Initial and the instruction executes.
RULE 69: When executing an instruction to disable unit, if the status in the mstatus register is Off, the next state is Off and the instruction executes.
RULE 70: When executing an instruction to disable unit, if the status in the mstatus register is Initial, the next state is Off and the instruction executes.
RULE 71: When executing an instruction to disable unit, if the status in the mstatus register is Clean, the next state is Off and the instruction executes.
RULE 72: When executing an instruction to disable unit, if the status in the mstatus register is Dirty, the next state is Off and the instruction executes.
RULE 73: When executing an instruction to enable unit, if the status in the mstatus register is Off, the next state is Initial and the instruction executes.
RULE 74: When executing an instruction to enable unit, if the status in the mstatus register is Initial, the next state is Initial and the instruction executes.
RULE 75: When executing an instruction to enable unit, if the status in the mstatus register is Clean, the next state is Initial and the instruction executes.
RULE 76: When executing an instruction to enable unit, if the status in the mstatus register is Dirty, the next state is Initial and the instruction executes.

The following description:

The `mnstatus` CSR holds a two-bit field, `MNPP`, which on entry to the RNMI trap handler holds the privilege mode of the interrupted context, encoded in the same manner as `mstatus.MPP`.

The `mnstatus` CSR holds a one-bit field, `MNPV`, which on entry to the RNMI trap handler holds the virtualization mode of the interrupted context, encoded in the same manner as `mstatus.MPV`.

When `NMIE=0`, the hart behaves as though `mstatus.MPRV` were clear, regardless of the current setting of `mstatus.MPRV`. (Note: `NMIE` is a bit in the `mnstatus` register).
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The mstatus register contains a field MPP which encodes the privilege mode of the interrupted context.
RULE 2: The mstatus register contains a field MPV which encodes the virtualization mode of the interrupted context.
RULE 3: When the NMIE bit in the mnstatus register is 0, the hart behaves as though the mstatus.MPRV field in the mstatus register were clear, regardless of the current setting of the mstatus.MPRV field.

The sstatus register is a subset of the mstatus register. In a straightforward implementation, reading or writing any field in sstatus is equivalent to reading or writing the homonymous field in mstatus.

The sstatus register is an SXLEN-bit read/write register formatted as shown in Figure 49 when SXLEN=32 and Figure 50 when SXLEN=64.

Figure 49. Supervisor-mode status ( sstatus ) register when SXLEN=32.

| 31      | 30      |      |      |         | 25   | 24   | 23    | 22   |      | 20   | 19   | 18   | 17   | 16      |
|---------|---------|------|------|---------|------|------|-------|------|------|------|------|------|---------|
| SD      | WPRI    | WPRI | WPRI | WPRI    |      | SDT  | SPELP |      | WPRI |      | MXR  | SUM  | WPRI | XS[1:0] |
| 15      | 13 14   | WPRI |      | VS[1:0] |      | SPP  | WPRI  | UBE  | SPIE |      | WPRI |      | SIE  | WPRI    |

Figure 50. Supervisor-mode status ( sstatus ) register when SXLEN=64.

The SPP bit indicates the privilege level at which a hart was executing before entering supervisor mode. When a trap is taken, SPP is set to 0 if the trap originated from user mode, or 1 otherwise. When an SRET instruction is executed to return from the trap handler, the privilege level is set to user mode if the SPP bit is 0, or supervisor mode if the SPP bit is 1; SPP is then set to 0.

The SIE bit enables or disables all interrupts in supervisor mode. When SIE is clear, interrupts are not taken while in supervisor mode. When the hart is running in user-mode, the value in SIE is ignored, and supervisor-level interrupts are enabled.

The SPIE bit indicates whether supervisor interrupts were enabled prior to trapping into supervisor mode. When a trap is taken into supervisor mode, SPIE is set to SIE, and SIE is set to 0. When an SRET instruction is executed, SIE is set to SPIE, then SPIE is set to 1.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The sstatus register is a subset of the mstatus register.
RULE 2: In a straightforward implementation, reading or writing any field in sstatus is equivalent to reading or writing the homonymous field in mstatus.
RULE 3: The SPP bit in mstatus indicates the privilege level at which a hart was executing before entering supervisor mode.
RULE 4: When a trap is taken, the SPP bit in mstatus is set to 0 if the trap originated from user mode.
RULE 5: When a trap is taken, the SPP bit in mstatus is set to 1 if the trap did not originate from user mode.
RULE 6: When an SRET instruction is executed to return from the trap handler, the privilege level is set to user mode if the SPP bit in mstatus is 0.
RULE 7: When an SRET instruction is executed to return from the trap handler, the privilege level is set to supervisor mode if the SPP bit in mstatus is 1.
RULE 8: When an SRET instruction is executed to return from the trap handler, the SPP bit in mstatus is set to 0.
RULE 9: The SIE bit in mstatus enables or disables all interrupts in supervisor mode.
RULE 10: When the SIE bit in mstatus is clear, interrupts are not taken while in supervisor mode.
RULE 11: When the hart is running in user-mode, the value in the SIE bit of mstatus is ignored, and supervisor-level interrupts are enabled.
RULE 12: The SPIE bit in mstatus indicates whether supervisor interrupts were enabled prior to trapping into supervisor mode.
RULE 13: When a trap is taken into supervisor mode, the SPIE bit in mstatus is set to the value of the SIE bit in mstatus.
RULE 14: When a trap is taken into supervisor mode, the SIE bit in mstatus is set to 0.
RULE 15: When an SRET instruction is executed, the SIE bit in mstatus is set to the value of the SPIE bit in mstatus.
RULE 16: When an SRET instruction is executed, the SPIE bit in mstatus is set to 1.
======================================================= END - 33 =======================================================

The following description:

The hypervisor extension adds two fields, MPV and GVA, to the machine-level mstatus or mstatush CSR, and modifies the behavior of several existing mstatus fields.

*   When MXLEN=64, the hypervisor extension adds MPV and GVA to mstatus.
*   When MXLEN=32, the hypervisor extension adds MPV and GVA not to mstatus but to mstatush.
*   For RV32, the format of mstatus is unchanged.

MPV Bit (Machine Previous Virtualization Mode)
*   The MPV bit is written by the implementation whenever a trap is taken into M-mode.
*   The MPV bit is set to the value of the virtualization mode V at the time of the trap.
*   When an MRET instruction is executed, the virtualization mode V is set to MPV, unless MPP=3, in which case V remains 0.

GVA Field (Guest Virtual Address)
*   Field GVA is written by the implementation whenever a trap is taken into M-mode.
*   For any trap (breakpoint, address misaligned, access fault, page fault, or guest-page fault) that writes a guest virtual address to mtval, GVA is set to 1.
*   For any other trap into M-mode, GVA is set to 0.

TSR, TVM, and TW Fields
*   The TSR and TVM fields of mstatus affect execution only in HS-mode, not in VS-mode.
*   The TW field affects execution in all modes except M-mode.
*   Setting TVM=1 prevents HS-mode from accessing hgatp or executing HFENCE.GVMA or HINVAL.GVMA.
*   Setting TVM=1 has no effect on accesses to vsatp or instructions HFENCE.VVMA or HINVAL.VVMA.
*   Setting TVM=1 has no effect on any actions taken in VS-mode.

MPRV Field (Modify Privilege)
*   The hypervisor extension changes the behavior of the Modify Privilege field, MPRV, of mstatus.
*   When MPRV=0, translation and protection behave as normal.
*   When MPRV=1, explicit memory accesses are translated and protected, and endianness is applied, as though the current virtualization mode were set to MPV and the current nominal privilege mode were set to MPP.

Table 49. Effect of MPRV on the translation and protection of explicit memory accesses.

| MPRV | MPV | MPP | Effect |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | - | - | Normal access; current privilege mode applies. |
| 1 | 0 | 0 | U-level access with HS-level translation and protection only. |
| 1 | 0 | 1 | HS-level access with HS-level translation and protection only. |
| 1 | - | 3 | M-level access with no translation. |
| 1 | 1 | 0 | VU-level access with two-stage translation and protection. The HS-levelMXR bit makes any executable page readable. vsstatus.MXR makes readable those pages marked executable at the VS translation stage, but only if readable at the guest-physical translation stage. |
| 1 | 1 | 1 | VS-level access with two-stage translation and protection. The HS-levelMXR bit makes any executable page readable. vsstatus.MXR makes readable those pages marked executable at the VS translation stage, but only if readable at the guest-physical translation stage. vsstatus.SUM applies instead of the HS-level SUMbit. |

Virtual-Machine Load/Store Instructions
*   MPRV does not affect the virtual-machine load/store instructions, HLV, HLVX, and HSV.
*   The explicit loads and stores of these instructions always act as though V=1 and the nominal privilege mode were hstatus.SPVP, overriding MPRV.

Register Relationships
*   The mstatus register is a superset of the HS-level sstatus register.
*   The mstatus register is not a superset of vsstatus.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: When MXLEN=64, the hypervisor extension adds the MPV and GVA fields to the mstatus register.
RULE 2: When MXLEN=32, the hypervisor extension adds the MPV and GVA fields to the mstatush register, not the mstatus register.
RULE 3: For RV32, the format of the mstatus register is unchanged.
RULE 4: The implementation writes the MPV bit of the mstatus register whenever a trap is taken into M-mode.
RULE 5: The implementation sets the MPV bit of the mstatus register to the value of the virtualization mode V at the time of the trap.
RULE 6: When an MRET instruction is executed, the virtualization mode V is set to the MPV bit of the mstatus register, unless MPP=3, in which case V remains 0.
RULE 7: The implementation writes the GVA field of the mstatus register whenever a trap is taken into M-mode.
RULE 8: The GVA field of the mstatus register is set to 1 for any trap (breakpoint, address misaligned, access fault, page fault, or guest-page fault) that writes a guest virtual address to mtval.
RULE 9: The GVA field of the mstatus register is set to 0 for any other trap into M-mode.
RULE 10: The TSR and TVM fields of the mstatus register affect execution only in HS-mode and not in VS-mode.
RULE 11: The TW field of the mstatus register affects execution in all modes except M-mode.
RULE 12: Setting the TVM field of the mstatus register to 1 prevents HS-mode from accessing hgatp or executing HFENCE.GVMA or HINVAL.GVMA.
RULE 13: Setting the TVM field of the mstatus register to 1 has no effect on accesses to vsatp or instructions HFENCE.VVMA or HINVAL.VVMA.
RULE 14: Setting the TVM field of the mstatus register to 1 has no effect on any actions taken in VS-mode.
RULE 15: The hypervisor extension changes the behavior of the Modify Privilege field, MPRV, of mstatus.
RULE 16: When the MPRV field of the mstatus register is 0, translation and protection behave as normal.
RULE 17: When the MPRV field of the mstatus register is 1, explicit memory accesses are translated and protected, and endianness is applied, as though the current virtualization mode were set to MPV and the current nominal privilege mode were set to MPP.
RULE 18: When the MPRV field of the mstatus register is 1, the MPV bit of the mstatus register is 0, and MPP is 0, explicit memory accesses result in U-level access with HS-level translation and protection only.
RULE 19: When the MPRV field of the mstatus register is 1, the MPV bit of the mstatus register is 0, and MPP is 1, explicit memory accesses result in HS-level access with HS-level translation and protection only.
RULE 20: When the MPRV field of the mstatus register is 1, the MPV bit of the mstatus register is unspecified, and MPP is 3, explicit memory accesses result in M-level access with no translation.
RULE 21: When the MPRV field of the mstatus register is 1, the MPV bit of the mstatus register is 1, and MPP is 0, explicit memory accesses result in VU-level access with two-stage translation and protection, where the HS-levelMXR bit makes any executable page readable and vsstatus.MXR makes readable those pages marked executable at the VS translation stage, but only if readable at the guest-physical translation stage.
RULE 22: When the MPRV field of the mstatus register is 1, the MPV bit of the mstatus register is 1, and MPP is 1, explicit memory accesses result in VS-level access with two-stage translation and protection, where the HS-levelMXR bit makes any executable page readable, vsstatus.MXR makes readable those pages marked executable at the VS translation stage, but only if readable at the guest-physical translation stage, and vsstatus.SUM applies instead of the HS-level SUM bit.
RULE 23: The MPRV field of the mstatus register does not affect the virtual-machine load/store instructions HLV, HLVX, and HSV.
RULE 24: The explicit loads and stores of the virtual-machine load/store instructions HLV, HLVX, and HSV always act as though V=1 and the nominal privilege mode were hstatus.SPVP, overriding the MPRV field of the mstatus register.
RULE 25: The mstatus register is a superset of the HS-level sstatus register.
RULE 26: The mstatus register is not a superset of vsstatus.
======================================================= END - 42 =======================================================

The following description:

* An instruction is HS-qualified if it would be valid to execute in HS-mode (for some values of the instruction's register operands), assuming fields TSR and TVM of CSR mstatus are both zero.
* When V=1, a virtual-instruction exception (code 22) is normally raised instead of an illegal-instruction exception if the attempted instruction is HS-qualified but is prevented from executing when V=1 either due to insufficient privilege or because the instruction is expressly disabled by a supervisor or hypervisor CSR such as scounteren or hcounteren.
* In VS-mode or VU-mode, a virtual-instruction exception is raised for attempts to access an implemented non-high-half hypervisor CSR or VS CSR when the same access (read/write) would be allowed in HS-mode, assuming mstatus.TVM=0.
* In VS-mode or VU-mode, if XLEN=32, a virtual-instruction exception is raised for attempts to access an implemented high-half hypervisor CSR or high-half VS CSR when the same access (read/write) to the CSR's low-half partner would be allowed in HS-mode, assuming mstatus.TVM=0.
* In VU-mode, a virtual-instruction exception is raised for attempts to execute WFI when mstatus.TW=0.
* In VU-mode, a virtual-instruction exception is raised for attempts to access an implemented non-high-half supervisor CSR when the same access (read/write) would be allowed in HS-mode, assuming mstatus.TVM=0.
* In VU-mode, if XLEN=32, a virtual-instruction exception is raised for attempts to access an implemented high-half supervisor CSR when the same access to the CSR's low-half partner would be allowed in HS-mode, assuming mstatus.TVM=0.
* In VS-mode, a virtual-instruction exception is raised for attempts to execute WFI when hstatus.VTW=1 and mstatus.TW=0, unless the instruction completes within an implementation-specific, bounded time.
* TSR and TVM in mstatus are intended to impact only S-mode (HS-mode).
* TSR and TVM in mstatus are ignored for determining exceptions in VS-mode.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: An instruction is HS-qualified if it would be valid to execute in HS-mode (for some values of the instruction's register operands), assuming fields TSR and TVM of CSR mstatus are both zero.
RULE 2: When V=1, a virtual-instruction exception (code 22) is normally raised instead of an illegal-instruction exception if the attempted instruction is HS-qualified (meaning it would be valid to execute in HS-mode for some values of the instruction's register operands, assuming fields TSR and TVM of CSR mstatus are both zero) but is prevented from executing when V=1 either due to insufficient privilege or because the instruction is expressly disabled by a supervisor or hypervisor CSR such as scounteren or hcounteren.
RULE 3: In VS-mode or VU-mode, a virtual-instruction exception is raised for attempts to access an implemented non-high-half hypervisor CSR or VS CSR when the same access (read/write) would be allowed in HS-mode, assuming mstatus.TVM=0.
RULE 4: In VS-mode or VU-mode, if XLEN=32, a virtual-instruction exception is raised for attempts to access an implemented high-half hypervisor CSR or high-half VS CSR when the same access (read/write) to the CSR's low-half partner would be allowed in HS-mode, assuming mstatus.TVM=0.
RULE 5: In VU-mode, a virtual-instruction exception is raised for attempts to execute WFI when mstatus.TW=0.
RULE 6: In VU-mode, a virtual-instruction exception is raised for attempts to access an implemented non-high-half supervisor CSR when the same access (read/write) would be allowed in HS-mode, assuming mstatus.TVM=0.
RULE 7: In VU-mode, if XLEN=32, a virtual-instruction exception is raised for attempts to access an implemented high-half supervisor CSR when the same access to the CSR's low-half partner would be allowed in HS-mode, assuming mstatus.TVM=0.
RULE 8: In VS-mode, a virtual-instruction exception is raised for attempts to execute WFI when hstatus.VTW=1 and mstatus.TW=0, unless the instruction completes within an implementation-specific, bounded time.
RULE 9: The TSR and TVM fields in mstatus are intended to impact only S-mode (HS-mode).
RULE 10: The TSR and TVM fields in mstatus are ignored for determining exceptions in VS-mode.
======================================================= END - 43 =======================================================

The following description:

When a trap is taken into M-mode, fields MPV and MPP in mstatus (or mstatush) are set according to Table 52.

When a trap is taken into M-mode, field GVA in mstatus / mstatush is written.

When a trap is taken into M-mode, field MPIE in mstatus / mstatush is written.

When a trap is taken into M-mode, field MIE in mstatus / mstatush is written.

Upon trap return, MPV is ignored when MPP=3.

Table 52. Value of mstatus / mstatush fields MPV and MPP after a trap into M-mode. Upon trap return, MPV is ignored when MPP=3.

| Previous Mode | MPV | MPP |
|---------------|-----|-----|
| U-mode         | 0   | 0   |
| HS-mode        | 0   | 1   |
| M-mode         | 0   | 3   |
| VU-mode        | 1   | 0   |
| VS-mode        | 1   | 1   |

- If Previous Mode is U-mode, MPV is 0 and MPP is 0.
- If Previous Mode is HS-mode, MPV is 0 and MPP is 1.
- If Previous Mode is M-mode, MPV is 0 and MPP is 3.
- If Previous Mode is VU-mode, MPV is 1 and MPP is 0.
- If Previous Mode is VS-mode, MPV is 1 and MPP is 1.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: When a trap is taken into M-mode, the GVA field in mstatus or mstatush is written.
RULE 2: When a trap is taken into M-mode, the MPIE field in mstatus or mstatush is written.
RULE 3: When a trap is taken into M-mode, the MIE field in mstatus or mstatush is written.
RULE 4: Upon trap return, the MPV field in mstatus or mstatush is ignored when the MPP field in mstatus or mstatush is 3.
RULE 5: When a trap is taken into M-mode from U-mode, the MPV field in mstatus or mstatush is set to 0 and the MPP field in mstatus or mstatush is set to 0.
RULE 6: When a trap is taken into M-mode from HS-mode, the MPV field in mstatus or mstatush is set to 0 and the MPP field in mstatus or mstatush is set to 1.
RULE 7: When a trap is taken into M-mode from M-mode, the MPV field in mstatus or mstatush is set to 0 and the MPP field in mstatus or mstatush is set to 3.
RULE 8: When a trap is taken into M-mode from VU-mode, the MPV field in mstatus or mstatush is set to 1 and the MPP field in mstatus or mstatush is set to 0.
RULE 9: When a trap is taken into M-mode from VS-mode, the MPV field in mstatus or mstatush is set to 1 and the MPP field in mstatus or mstatush is set to 1.

MRET instruction:
